### Part 5 Detailed Content (Lines 1–707 of part5of12.txt)

**Cantos XCIX–CIII (Bharat reaches Chitrakuta; funeral libation):** Bharat's army surrounds the hill. Bharat goes alone on foot to Rama's leaf cottage. The brothers meet. Rama learns from Bharat that Dasharatha is dead. The brothers go to the Mandakini river to perform the funeral water-libation — the specific rite for a father who has died in the carrier's absence. The libation is done in silence.

**Cantos CIV–CXI (The debate over Rama's return):** Bharat pleads five times across multiple cantos for Rama to return and rule. Rama refuses each time, citing one principle: the king's word is a sacred debt that the son cannot dissolve. Between the pleas, the philosopher Javalin makes a full atheist argument to Rama — gods do not exist, the afterlife is a priestly invention, dead men have no will and living oaths to them have no force; therefore Rama should return. Javalin argues this explicitly and at length. Rama rejects it entirely: truth is the highest dharma, not the living man's comfort. He says he could sooner lose Lakshmana, Sita, or his own life than break a promise. Javalin immediately retracts — he only spoke thus to test Rama's resolution. Vasishtha then recites the full genealogy of Rama's lineage from Brahma through Manu and Ikshvaku to Dasharatha — the recitation that certifies Rama's fitness to be exactly as bound by duty as he is. Bharat's final refusal is accepted.

**Canto CXII (The Sandals as regency):** Bharat asks Rama to place his golden sandals on Bharat's feet. Rama places them. Bharat removes them and places them on the royal elephant's head. He declares publicly: these sandals will be installed on the throne of Ayodhya; all state business will be conducted in their name; he will rule only as their agent; if Rama does not return on the fourteenth year's last day, Bharat will enter fire.

**Cantos CXIII–CXV (Nandigram; Bharat as regent):** Bharat departs with the sandals installed on the throne at Nandigram, outside Ayodhya proper. He wears hermit dress, grows matted hair, eats only fruit. Every state petition is laid before the sandals first. He refers all decisions to them. The city of Ayodhya is described: silent, dark, cats and owls roaming empty streets, music and flowers gone, the river pools dying. The description is one of sustained civic grief.

**Canto CXVI (The hermits' warning; demons in Dandaka):** The hermits near Chitrakuta tell Rama that Khara's demons (Ravana's brother's forces) have been intensifying attacks on the forest saints — rending bodies, extinguishing sacred fires, defiling altars. The hermits cannot curse the demons without losing all their accumulated penance-merit. They ask Rama to protect them or depart. The saints then leave their own hermitage. Rama refuses to leave — he will protect them.

**Canto CXVII (Atri's hermitage; Anasuyá's teaching):** Rama and party move to the hermitage of the sage Atri. Atri's wife Anasuyá — a very ancient female devotee, white-haired, shaking — receives Sita. She gives Sita an extended teaching on wifely virtue: the woman who follows her husband through hardship is elevated; even a wicked or faithless husband is the wife's proper deity; Sita who follows Rama is the highest type.

**Canto CXVIII (Anasuyá's gifts; Sita tells her own story):** Sita recounts to Anasuyá how she was found in the ritual furrow, how Janak raised her, how the bow trial worked. Anasuyá rewards Sita's virtue with three celestial gifts: a divine robe of heavenly fabric, ornamental gems, and a balsam that will make her limbs shine with permanent beauty — "as Lakshmi's beauty lends grace to Vishnu's face." Sita puts the robe on; she returns to Rama glowing.

**Canto CXIX / Book III, Canto I (Dandaka Forest; the hermit settlement):** They enter the great Dandaka Forest. A hermit settlement blazes with ascetic radiance. The hermits formally acknowledge Rama as their king — citing the principle that the king receives one-quarter of a subject's penance-merit in exchange for protection. They ask him to guard them.

**Cantos II–IV (Viradha; burial as killing):** A giant named Viradha seizes Sita. Arrows pass through him without killing him — he has a boon from Brahma that no weapon can pierce his body. Rama and Lakshmana fight him, break both his arms, and realize the only way to kill someone whose body cannot be killed by weapons is to bury him alive. They dig a pit and press him into it. Viradha, dying underground, reveals his real identity: he is Tumburu, a celestial musician cursed by Kubera for falling in love with the apsara Rambha. Rama has freed him; he departs to heaven.

**Canto V (Sarabhanga's self-immolation):** At the hermitage of Sarabhanga, Indra is departing as Rama arrives — Indra leaves quickly, saying Rama is not yet ready to meet him. Sarabhanga welcomes Rama, then says he has been holding back his liberation solely to see Rama. He lights his own sacred fire, lays his body in it, and burns away; from the flame he emerges transformed — young, radiant — and ascends beyond the realm of gods to Brahma's own sphere.

**Canto VI (Rama's promise to the hermits):** The hermit multitude — listed in precise taxonomic categories by how they do penance (standing on one foot, eating only air, lying in pools, bearing five fires, sleeping on bare earth) — flock to Rama. They show him the bodies of slaughtered saints strewn near Chitrakuta. Rama formally promises to protect them all and destroy the demons who prey on them.

**Cantos VII–VIII (Sutikshna; ten years compressed):** Rama visits the sage Sutikshna. Sutikshna offers him all the worlds won by his penance; Rama declines, asking only for a pleasant spot to rest. He is reluctant to linger because his weapons would threaten the tame animals the hermit has tamed. He departs with Sutikshna's blessing to tour all the hermitages of Dandaka. Ten years pass in a single stanza — Rama spends months in each hermitage, weeks in others, moving through the forest visiting every saint.

**Canto IX (Sita's Speech; the fable of Sage Suchi):** Sita warns Rama about one of the three sins: killing without cause. She tells the fable of Sage Suchi — a forest hermit who kept Indra's abandoned sword "just to guard it, for someone might take it." Day by day, as he carried the sword, his penance-virtue drained. He became warlike, violent, fell to hell. The sword corrupted him through proximity, not intent. She argues that Rama, carrying weapons through the forest, risks the same fate. She urges him to put the bow down when not fighting, to be a hermit and not a warrior while in exile.

**Canto X (Rama's reply):** Rama acknowledges Sita's speech as coming from love. He says he could endure losing Lakshmana, losing Sita, losing his own life — but not breaking a promise to Brahmans who came to him in distress. The hermits asked him and he swore. The oath is sacred.

**Cantos XI–XII (Agastya; the demon Vatapi digested; the divine bow):** Sutikshna directs Rama to the great sage Agastya. On the way, Rama tells Lakshmana how Agastya previously defeated the demons Vatapi and Ilval who had been killing Brahmans — Ilval would invite Brahmans to a funeral feast, serve them Vatapi disguised as meat, then call out "Vatapi, come forth!" and Vatapi would rip out of the guest's stomach. Agastya simply ate Vatapi and digested him; when Ilval called out, Agastya patted his belly and said "where will he come from?" Then he killed Ilval with a glance. Agastya receives Rama with great joy. He gives Rama three items: the divine bow made for Vishnu by the Heavenly Artificer, a quiver with inexhaustible arrows, and a golden sword. He directs them to Panchavati.

**Canto XIII (Agastya's counsel; direction to Panchavati):** Agastya praises Sita's faithfulness at length ("not so other women...") and commends her as like Arundhati the paragon. He tells Rama to make his home at Panchavati on the Godavari river — a pleasant spot, safe, beautiful, where Sita will be happy.

**Canto XIV (Jatayu; the genealogy of all creation):** On the road to Panchavati, a vast vulture confronts them. The princes are alarmed but the vulture speaks gently: "Know me as your father's old friend." The vulture gives his genealogy — an account of all creation from Kashyap's wives and daughters through every species of living thing (the specific maternal lineages of kine, serpents, horses, demons, birds, deer, elephants, and men). At the end: he is Jatayu, son of Shyeni, Dasharatha's lifelong ally. He offers to guard Sita when Rama and Lakshmana are away hunting. Rama embraces him.

**Canto XV (Panchavati established):** They arrive at Panchavati. Rama describes the site to Lakshmana: Godavari visible through the trees, deer in hundreds, peacocks crying from every hill, the Sal tree and the Mango and the Champac and the Ashoka all in bloom. Lakshmana builds a cottage. Rama praises it, embraces Lakshmana, says "our father lives again in you."

**Canto XVI (Winter; Lakshmana's meditation on Bharat):** Winter arrives; they go each morning to bathe in the Godavari. Lakshmana delivers an extended description of winter — hoarfrost, weak sun, cold water that elephants pull back from, the star Rohini dim as a widow, the barley bending under its own ripeness. Then he speaks of Bharat at Nandigram: sleeping on cold earth, eating little, living in hermit dress, holding the kingdom only for Rama. Rama is moved. He says: "I would give more than the throne to see Bharat again, and Shatrughna."

**Cantos XVII–XVIII (Surpanakha arrives; the mutilation):** A giantess named Surpanakha arrives at their cottage — she is Ravana's sister, daughter of the same mother. She sees Rama and is overcome with desire. Rama deflects her to Lakshmana; Lakshmana teases her back to Rama; she threatens to eat Sita so she can have Rama freely. Rama orders Lakshmana to mark her. Lakshmana cuts off her nose and ears. She flies screaming to Janasthan, to her brother Khara and his giant army.

**Cantos XIX–XXV (The rousing of Khara; 14,000 giants; battle begins):** Surpanakha tells Khara; Khara sends 14 giants who are killed. Surpanakha returns; Khara assembles 14,000 giants and his war-chief Dushana. Bad omens accompany the march: blood rains, vultures, eclipses, meteors. Khara dismisses all omens. The army approaches Panchavati. Rama sends Lakshmana and Sita into a cave and prepares alone, in armor. The gods assemble in the sky to watch. The battle begins — Part 5 ends with Rama releasing thousands of arrows into the advancing host, killing hundreds, but the battle not yet concluded.

---

### Part 6 Detailed Content (Lines 1–612 of part6of12.txt)

**Cantos XXV–XXX (Battle conclusion; Khara's death):** Rama kills Dushana — severs both arms, Dushana crashes like a mountain. He kills the three captains (Trisiras among them, by shooting each of his three heads with a separate shaft). Khara alone faces Rama; they exchange formal speeches: Rama on the dharma of punishment, Khara on warrior pride. Khara hurls a Sal tree uprooted from the ground; Rama splits it in flight. Rama uses the Brahma's Staff arrow (given by Indra at the world's founding); it kills Khara. In three hours, Rama on foot kills 14,000 giants plus three commanders. The gods celebrate from the sky. The hermit saints come out to praise Rama.

**Canto XXXI (Ravana learns of the disaster):** One survivor, Akampana, flies to Lanka and reports the disaster to Ravana. Ravana's fury. Akampana describes Rama's strength in detail, then advises: "You cannot beat him in direct fight. Steal his wife — a man bereft of his beloved will waste away and die of grief."

**Cantos XXXII–XXXV (Surpanakha's political lecture; Ravana decides):** Surpanakha goes to Ravana's full court and delivers a speech that is simultaneously a political lecture and a personal attack: the king who neglects his spies, who does not know the state of his own realm, who is absorbed in pleasure while his subordinates are destroyed, will lose everything. She formally catalogues Ravana's failures as a monarch. Then she pivots to Sita — describing her beauty in extravagant terms (no apsara, nymph, goddess or Gandharva wife can match her) and arguing that Ravana should make Sita his queen. Ravana makes his decision. He flies his demon-assed chariot to the ocean shore, then to Maricha's hermitage. Extended descriptions of the ocean shore — its trees, birds, hermits, gandharvas, nymphs, sacred spaces.

**Cantos XXXVI–XL (Maricha's three speeches against the plan):** Ravana explains the deer-decoy plan. Maricha gives three long speeches opposing it, each stronger than the last. He gives his own history: as a young giant rampaging in the forest, he was struck by one arrow from the teenage Rama and thrown 100 leagues into the sea — but spared. Since that day he has seen Rama's face in every tree, every shrub, every shadow. He sleeps and dreams Rama. He wakes in terror at sounds that begin with the letter R. He says the plan will destroy all the giants and Lanka itself. Ravana offers him a choice: do this or die by my hand now. Maricha yields, saying "death by Rama would be better than death by you — but I have no choice."

**Cantos XLI–XLIV (The golden deer; Maricha's death):** Maricha transforms into a supernatural deer of impossible beauty: golden hide with silver drops, sapphire-tipped antlers, ruby ears, emerald-and-almandine forehead, rainbow tail, hooves of carved blue stone. A light goes before him wherever he walks. He wanders near Sita's cottage. Sita sees him and is enchanted. She calls Rama and Lakshmana. Lakshmana suspects it is Maricha in disguise; Sita insists on having the creature. Rama decides: if it is a real deer, the skin is a treasure; if it is Maricha, he will kill him as he was supposed to long ago. He chases the deer for hours through the forest, the deer appearing and vanishing. Finally he shoots it. Maricha, dying, cries out in a perfect imitation of Rama's voice: "O Sita! O Lakshmana!" He resumes his giant form and dies. Rama realizes the trap.

**Cantos XLV–XLVI (Lakshmana leaves; Ravana arrives):** Sita hears the cry, believes it is Rama in distress, and orders Lakshmana to go. He refuses repeatedly — explaining that Rama cannot be in danger, that the voice was demonic mimicry, that leaving Sita alone is exactly what the demons want. Sita escalates: she accuses him of wanting Rama dead so he can have her; of being sent by Bharat to observe Rama's death. Lakshmana leaves, furious, his hands raised in salute. Ravana arrives at the empty cottage in the guise of a wandering brahmin mendicant — sandaled feet, red robe, staff, water-pot. The text marks the moment: every tree stops moving, the river goes silent, the wind dies. Ravana praises Sita's beauty at length, then questions her. She tells him the full story of her life: her birth from the ritual furrow, the bow trial, the exile, why they are in the forest. She asks him to identify himself.

**Cantos XLVII–XLIX (Ravana's proposal; the abduction; ornaments fall):** Ravana reveals himself as the ten-necked king of Lanka. He proposes marriage. Sita refuses with a long speech of contemptuous comparisons: Rama is to Ravana as gold is to lead, as the swan is to a crow, as the peacock is to an owl, as the sun is to a firefly. Ravana threatens her: if she does not accept within twelve months, he will mince her flesh for his morning meal. He seizes her and flies. The natural world witnesses: the sun dims, the wind stops, the river mourns, the birds freeze. Brahma, watching from the sky, says "the deed is done — this was ordained." As Ravana flies north to Lanka, Sita's ornaments fall: the garland, anklet bracelet, earrings, necklace, scarf. She sees five monkeys on a hilltop and drops her remaining scarf and jewelry onto them deliberately — hoping they'll tell Rama where she went.

**Cantos L–LI (Jatayu fights Ravana; falls):** Jatayu the ancient vulture king challenges Ravana from a treetop. He recites the law of kings (do not touch other men's wives) and asks Ravana to release Sita. He fights. He breaks Ravana's bow three times, kills his ass-headed steeds, destroys his chariot. He tears ten of Ravana's arms away — but they regenerate instantly. Old and weakened, Jatayu's wings are finally cut by Ravana's sword. He falls to earth, bleeding.

**Cantos LIV–LV (Lanka; Sita in the Ashoka grove; Ravana's twelve-month ultimatum):** Ravana places Sita in a bower in the royal palace, surrounded by she-demons instructed to watch her. He shows her the wealth of Lanka: golden pillars, diamond latticework, ivory staircases, gardens, lakes. He proposes again, prostrating his ten heads at her feet. She refuses. His final ultimatum: twelve months. He leaves. The she-demons take her to the Ashoka grove — the most beautiful garden in Lanka — where she is guarded and weeps.

**Canto LVII (Indra visits Sita with celestial food):** Brahma sends Indra to comfort Sita. Indra puts all the giants of Lanka to sleep. He appears to Sita. She demands proof he is truly Indra — feet off the ground, unwinking eyes, unfading garland, garment without dust. He demonstrates all four. He gives her food made from Indra's own provisions — she will not hunger or thirst for as long as she holds its virtue. She eats, dedicating the merit to Rama and Lakshmana. Indra leaves.

**Cantos LVIII–LXVII (Rama discovers the loss; the great lament):** Rama returns to the empty cottage. He searches frantically — calling to each tree and plant and animal by name ("O Ashoka, O Cassia, O spotted deer, where is my Sita?"). He finds the trail of scattered ornaments. He finds the wreckage of Ravana's chariot, the dead ass-headed horses, Ravana's broken bows. He descends into full grief, threatening to destroy the three worlds if Sita is not restored — calling on the sun, the wind, the river to testify. The river, the mountain, and the trees are described as wanting to speak but unable; they saw everything and fear Ravana. Lakshmana talks him down. They search south following the ornament trail. They find Jatayu mortally wounded on the ground. Part 6 ends with Rama approaching the fallen vulture, initially mistaking him for a demon.

---

### Part 7 Detailed Content (Lines 1–603 of part7of12.txt)

**Canto LXIX (Jatayu's death):** Jatayu, initially mistaken for a demon, is recognized. He gives his final account to Rama — confirms Ravana took Sita, describes the full fight (Jatayu broke Ravana's bow three times, killed the ass-headed steeds, tore ten of Ravana's arms which immediately regenerated, then had his wings cut), points south, says "him, brother of the Lord of Gold" (naming Ravana as Kubera's half-brother), and dies. Rama and Lakshmana perform the full royal funeral — hunt deer, gather wood, make death-food balls, recite the brahmin death texts, burn Jatayu's body on a pyre, pour libations in the Godavari.

**Canto LXX–LXXI (Kabandha):** They encounter a giantess Ayomukhi who tries to drag Lakshmana to be her husband; Lakshmana cuts off her nose and ears. Then: Kabandha — a headless trunk the size of a mountain, a face in his chest, arms a league long extending in every direction, seizing deer, bears, and birds from the air. He catches Rama and Lakshmana. Lakshmana argues they must cut off the arms. They do. Kabandha falls and recognizes them — reveals he is Danu, a divine being cursed into this form for frightening a sage (Sthulasiras), then further disfigured by Indra's bolt, his head and thighs driven into his chest. Indra gave him arms a league long so he could eat. Indra promised release when Rama burned him.

**Cantos LXXII–LXXIV (Kabandha burned; his counsel from the sky):** The brothers burn Kabandha's body in a pit. He rises from the fire in his beautiful divine form — golden ornaments, flashing jewels, a swan-drawn chariot. From the sky he tells Rama the route to find help: west through the fruit-laden groves, past Savari's hermitage, to Pampa lake, to Rishyamuka's hill where Sugriva the Vanar king lives in exile from his brother Bali. Kabandha describes the route in lavish detail — specific trees, the seven sacred lakes, the smell of Matanga's wood, the eastern cave where Sugriva dwells. He ascends to heaven.

**Canto LXXV (Savari):** They find Savari — an ancient female devotee, white-haired, shaking, who served Matanga's hermitage for generations. She had been told by the departing saints that Rama would come; she held back her liberation to receive him. She shows them the hermitage's surviving wonders: the seven lakes that moved themselves when the saints grew too old to walk to them, the bark coats still dripping from ancient baths, flowers dedicated by hermits still fresh after centuries. Rama gives her permission to depart. She burns herself, rises to heaven clothed in divine garments with celestial wreaths.

**Canto LXXVI / Book IV Canto I (Pampa; Rama's long lament):** They reach Pampa lake. Rama's full seasonal lament — the most extended grief speech in the epic so far. Every element of spring and the lovely landscape (the Koyil birds, peacocks dancing with their mates, creepers embracing trees, the mallard with his mate, bees drunk on lotus pollen, the Sal trees in flower) drives home that Sita is absent. He names each bird, each tree, each season-specific sight, and says that without Sita it is all ash. Lakshmana rouses him with a speech on zeal.

**Cantos II–IV (Sugriva's alarm; Hanuman's disguise):** The brothers climb toward Rishyamuka. Sugriva sees them and panics — he assumes Bali has sent disguised agents. The entire Vanar retinue scatters to higher peaks. Hanuman counsels Sugriva to investigate, not flee. Hanuman disguises himself as a wandering brahmin mendicant, approaches Rama and Lakshmana, and delivers a perfect formal speech of greeting — flawless grammar, correct grammar from all three registers, no tell of guile. Rama delivers a tutorial to Lakshmana on what excellent envoy-speech looks like (citing the Rig Veda, Yajush, and Saman — the listener must know all three, and know grammar; Hanuman's speech had consonants from all three speaking-registers, no pause, no wrong word).

**Cantos V–VI (The league; the ornaments):** Hanuman carries the brothers to Rishyamuka on his back. He presents them to Sugriva. Sugriva and Rama swear alliance before a fire made by Hanuman from wood-on-wood friction. Sugriva tells Rama he saw a woman being carried north by a demon, crying Rama's name. She dropped her scarf and jewelry onto a hilltop where five Vanars (including Sugriva) were watching. He shows Rama the gathered ornaments. Rama collapses — presses them to his chest, weeps. Lakshmana recognizes only the anklets (she wore them in front of him often); the earrings and bracelets are outside his knowledge.

**Cantos VIII–XI (Sugriva's story; Dundubhi):** Sugriva tells his full history. Their father's death; Bali becoming king; the demon Mayavi challenging Bali; Bali following him into a cave, instructing Sugriva to wait; a year of silence, then blood flowing from the cave; Sugriva assumes Bali dead, seals the cave with a boulder, returns to Kishkindha, is made king. Bali returns — furious. He expelled Sugriva, took his wife Ruma. Sugriva's chiefs were killed or imprisoned. Then: the Dundubhi story (backstory explaining why Bali dare not enter Rishyamuka). Dundubhi was a bull-demon who challenged Ocean and Himalaya for a fight; both sent him to Bali; Bali killed him, hurled the corse a league away. Blood drops from the flying body fell on Matanga's hermitage; Matanga cursed Bali: if Bali comes within one league of Rishyamuka, he dies.

**Canto XII (The palm trees test):** Sugriva asks Rama to prove his strength before trusting him. Rama fires one arrow: it pierces all seven palm trees in a row, passes through the hill behind them, passes through six underground realms, and returns to his quiver. Sugriva prostrates himself.

**Cantos XIII–XV (Kishkindha; Tara's warning ignored):** They march to Kishkindha. Sugriva shouts the challenge. Inside, Bali's queen Tara gives him wise counsel — she has heard from Angad their son that Rama and Lakshmana have allied with Sugriva; she knows Rama's strength; she begs Bali to make peace with his brother. Bali dismisses her, ties his warrior sash, rushes out. First fight: Rama cannot distinguish the brothers (they are identical in appearance, manner, and battle-shout), holds his arrow. Sugriva is badly beaten, flees to Rishyamuka. Lakshmana puts a flowering garland around Sugriva's neck as a distinguishing mark.

**Cantos XVI–XVII (Bali's fall; Bali's dying speech):** Second challenge. Bali ignores Tara's second warning. He fights Sugriva again. Rama shoots from hiding — the arrow pierces Bali's chest. Bali falls "like Indra's festival pole when they pull it down." His dying speech is a formal reproach to Rama — twenty stanzas of legal argument: Rama shot him from hiding, while fighting his own brother, without any prior injury from Bali, without warning, without jurisdiction over a Vanar king in his own forest, with no legal basis; this is how a hunter kills animals, not how a dharmic king acts; if Rama had simply asked, Bali would have retrieved Sita himself that same day and bound Ravana at Rama's feet.

**Canto XVIII (Rama's reply):** Rama gives a long formal counter-argument. Bali's offence: he took his own brother's wife Ruma and kept her. The law of Manu: kings must punish this. Under Bharat's rule, Rama enforces dharmic law across all territories. Hunting from ambush is legal and traditional for kings — even saints do it. Bali, as a Vanar, is categorically like a deer, and kings may shoot deer unseen. Bali accepts all of this, asks Rama to care for Angad, gives Sugriva the golden chain Indra gave him.

**Cantos XIX–XXIV (Tara's grief; Bali's funeral):** Tara's long lamentation — she was told, she warned him, he didn't listen, and now she watches the result she predicted. Hanuman tries to console her; she refuses all comfort except to sit beside Bali's body. Sugriva's guilt speech — he mourns that he used the device of Rama's power to kill his own brother; compares himself to Indra who killed Vishvarupa (a cosmic crime). Bali's elaborate funeral: a golden litter made by craftsmen is brought; Angad and Sugriva place Bali's body on it; carried to a sandy riverbank; a great pyre; Angad lights it; the Vanar retinue wails. Tara's final lament at the pyre — she cannot lay her head on Bali's chest because the arrow is still in his heart.

**Canto XXVI (Coronation; rain-season pause):** Sugriva is crowned in Kishkindha with oil, gems, umbrellas, chouries, sacred water from every river and sea — by the monkey chieftains. Angad is made junior co-regent. Rama cannot enter the city (vow to his father). He and Lakshmana settle in a cave on Mount Prasravana for the four-month rainy season.

**Cantos XXVII–XXIX (The rains; Sugriva's distraction; Hanuman's warning):** Rama's second long lament — seasonal, about rain this time. Every cloud is like Sita struggling in Ravana's arms; every lightning flash is like her thrown against dark clouds; every heavy downpour is the earth weeping for her. Sugriva, restored to wives and power, becomes absorbed in pleasure and forgets his promise entirely. Hanuman approaches him with a careful diplomatic speech — lists everything Rama has done for him, argues that repaying friendship is the core of noble kingship, warns that Rama will grow angry. Sugriva is stirred, orders Nila to summon all Vanar armies from every direction within fifteen days or face death.

**Canto XXX (Rama's autumn lament):** The rains clear. Rama stands on the mountain at night, in moonlight, thinking of Sita. Hears the Saras cranes call. He mourns that the season is beautiful and Sita isn't here to see it; wonders whether she, imprisoned, sees the same autumn sky and grieves. Part 7 ends mid-lament.

---

### Part 8 Detailed Content (Lines 1–813 of part8of12.txt)

**Cantos XXXI–XXXIV (Lakshmana's embassy to Sugriva; Tara's mediation):** Rama sends Lakshmana to rouse Sugriva, who has neglected his promised search for Sita. Lakshmana goes furious, hand on his bow. Angad meets him at the gate and escorts him carefully. Sugriva is with his wives, intoxicated, and delays receiving Lakshmana. Lakshmana's anger is described at length — he is ready to fire the city entirely. Tara intercedes: she speaks to Lakshmana with perfect formal courtesy, asks him to understand that men absorbed in pleasure surface slowly but are not evil, pledges that Sugriva will honor his alliance. She speaks privately to Sugriva — the threat is real; Lakshmana came ready to burn Kishkindha. Sugriva emerges contrite. He and Lakshmana embrace. Sugriva formally apologizes and orders the armies assembled immediately.

**Cantos XXXV–XL (Summoning the Vanar armies; the four search zones):** Sugriva issues orders through Nila, Jambavan, and his chieftains: every Vanar king from every direction must assemble within fifteen days or face death. The text describes the four search zones in extended geographical detail — east: through Vindhya and Deccan to the ocean shore, past specific rivers, mountains, lakes; west: through the Sindh plains to the western sea; north: through the Himalayan foothills to the snow regions; south: through Dandaka to Lanka. A hundred million Vanars and bear-fighters assemble on Kishkindha's plain — the names of the chieftains and their home territories are listed.

**Canto XLI (Rama's ring given to Hanuman):** Rama selects Hanuman for the southern search party. He gives Hanuman his own signet ring — his personal mark, inscribed with his name — as a token for Sita. If Sita is found, Hanuman will show her the ring to prove he comes from Rama. Hanuman places the ring on his finger and bows.

**Cantos XLII–XLVI (Four search parties; three return empty):** All four parties depart. The east, west, and north parties return before their deadlines — unsuccessful. The south party, led by Angad, searches every grove, cave, island shore, and lake through Dandaka and the southern jungles. They find no sign of Sita. Their deadline passes. Angad delivers a formal speech of despair — to return having failed means punishment by Sugriva; they cannot succeed; the only remaining option is death by fasting on the ocean shore. He proposes they all sit and not rise again. The party agrees and sits.

**Cantos XLVII–XLVIII (The cursed desert; Maya's enchanted cave; Svayamprabha):** Before the ocean, the south party crosses the desert of Kandu — the hermit Kandu, enraged at a heron, turned the land to waste. On the far edge they find a cave sealed by darkness. A kite leads them in. Inside: black trees leaved with gold, light without sun or fire, paths of gilded stone, a lake of golden lotuses with silver fish, halls hung with celestial garlands and preserved food. The cave is the old hermitage of the demon-architect Maya, now kept by his devotee Svayamprabha — an ascetic woman, white-haired, tending its perfection. She feeds all the Vanars. When they need to leave, she has them close their eyes; she leads them through; they open their eyes at the ocean shore. Svayamprabha then burns herself and ascends to Saubha's heaven.

**Cantos XLIX–LI (Angad's despair; Sampati arrives):** At the ocean shore Angad delivers his despair-speech again — past the deadline, no Sita, punishment certain on return. The party sits to fast. A vulture of enormous size descends from a sea-cliff above them. He has no wings — his wing-stumps are burned. He sees the Vanars prepared to die and says: "You will die and I will eat you. I have been without food for a long time." The Vanars speak among themselves, mentioning Rama and Jatayu. The vulture's expression changes entirely. He says: "Did you say Jatayu? He was my brother." His name is Sampati.

**Cantos LII–LV (Sampati's story):** Sampati tells his full history. In his youth he and Jatayu competed: who could fly closer to the sun? Both flew upward. Jatayu began to burn; Sampati spread his own wings over Jatayu to shield him, and the sun burned Sampati's wings entirely. Jatayu fell to earth (Part 7 covered his eventual death at Ravana's hands). Sampati fell to earth wingless at the Vindhya shore. A hermit named Nishakar found him and told him a prophecy: Sampati would recover his wings when he gave testimony to Rama's envoys searching for Sita. Sampati has been waiting, wingless, on the cliff. He also has information: he watched from his sea-cliff as Ravana flew north carrying a beautiful woman crying "Rama! Rama!" His son Suparsva, searching the southern seas, confirmed she is in Lanka — Ravana's island fortress — 100 leagues south across the open ocean.

**Cantos LVI–LVII (Sampati's wings regrow; the confirmation):** Having told his story, Sampati stands. His wings regrow before the Vanars' eyes — new feathers full-spread, the burnished color of his original plumage. The prophecy is fulfilled. He confirms the specifics: Sita is in Ravana's Ashoka grove, in the city of Lanka, 100 leagues south. He names the island's coastline shape and the direction. Then he flies — the first flight in a century — back to the cliff top.

**Cantos LVIII–LXI (Ocean shore council; who can leap 100 leagues):** The Vanars stand staring south at the horizon. Angad asks: who among them can leap 100 leagues? Each Vanar names his maximum range — 10, 20, 30, 40 leagues, in sequence. Jambavan names his own age — in his prime he circled the earth; now he can do 90 leagues but cannot land safely. Angad announces he can do 100 leagues, will do it himself. The party argues: a commander cannot take a risk that might leave them leaderless. Who then?

**Cantos LXII–LXIII (Jambavan rouses Hanuman):** Jambavan approaches Hanuman, who has been sitting silent at the group's edge. He delivers a formal speech of praise — recounting Hanuman's origins, birth-power, early exploits, and the reason he has been holding his strength back. He says: "Why do you sit here like an ordinary person? Don't you know what you are?" Hanuman begins to grow. He rises from the shore, grows to the size of a mountain, tail lashing. He says: he will leap to Lanka. He will land there as a cat.

**Cantos LXIV–LXV (Hanuman's origin):** Hanuman gives his own account. His mother Anjana was an apsara cursed into Vanar form. The Wind God passed her in a forest grove and Hanuman was born. As an infant he saw the rising sun and, thinking it a red fruit, leaped for it — 30,000 leagues in one morning jump. He grabbed Rahu (the eclipsing demon) by mistake. Rahu fled to Indra. Indra threw his thunderbolt. Hanuman fell on a mountain, his jaw broken (hanu = jaw; hence the name). The Wind God, furious, withdrew all wind from the world. Brahma, seeing the disaster, restored Hanuman and granted him immunity — no weapon would wound him unless he chose. Indra gave him one further gift: death only when he wills it.

**Book V, Canto I (The leap; Mainaka):** Hanuman compresses himself at the summit of a coastal mountain, then erupts from it — the mountaintop cracks from his launch force. The gods and Gandharvas assemble in the sky to watch. Mainaka — a mountain that was once winged, before Indra clipped all mountains' wings, and which now rests under the ocean — rises at Varuna's command to offer itself as a resting platform. Hanuman touches it briefly out of courtesy and does not land: no time.

**Cantos II–III (Surasa and Sinhika):** Surasa — a she-monster sent by the gods as a test — rises from the sea and declares that nothing may pass her without entering her mouth first. Hanuman grows; she grows to match him. He shrinks to thumb-size, enters her mouth, and exits before she can swallow. She releases him with praise: "Nothing can stop you." Farther south, Sinhika — a demon who feeds by catching shadows — reaches up from below the water's surface and grips Hanuman's shadow, stopping his flight. He senses the drag, dives, shrinks, enters Sinhika's open mouth, tears her vitals, bursts out. She dies. He continues.

**Canto IV (Lanka seen from the air):** He lands on the coastal peak of Lamba and sees Lanka: white walls, gold parapets, moats filled with lotus flowers, terraced gardens, flagged towers, streets paved with crystal. The city blazes like a cloud bank at sunset. Peacocks call from every spire. Hanuman thinks: this city is defended by more than walls. He must enter small.

**Cantos V–VII (Entry as a cat; the guardian goddess Lanka):** He shrinks to cat-size as night falls and approaches the city gate. The goddess Lanka — the city itself embodied — blocks his path: armored, terrible, the size of a thundercloud. Hanuman strikes her with his left fist. She falls. Rising, she speaks: Brahma gave her a prophecy — when she is struck down and falls, Lanka's end is near. She lets him pass.

**Cantos VIII–XII (Search through the palace district):** Moving through Lanka by night, Hanuman maps each quarter: Prahasta's house, Kumbhakarna's enormous courtyard, the generals' residences, the treasury. He enters Ravana's palace — the Pushpaka car is described: a celestial vehicle of living gold, capable of traveling at the speed of thought, shaped like a mountain with terraces and balconies, currently parked. He finds the ladies' bower — thousands of sleeping women in elaborate poetic detail: their jewels, loosened hair, garments, wine-flushed faces, sleeping attitudes.

**Canto XIII (Ravana asleep):** Hanuman finds Ravana's own chamber. Ravana lies asleep — dark as a thundercloud, arms like iron pillars, his chest bearing named battle-scars: the mark of Airavata's tusk from the fight with Indra, the mark of Kubera's mace. He breathes like a mountain. His ten heads are placed at rest. He is surrounded by the most beautiful women in the three worlds.

**Canto XIV (Mandodari; the correction):** Among the sleeping women, Hanuman sees one surpassing all the others in beauty — adorned with the finest ornaments, calm, radiant, at ease — and feels a surge of certainty: this is Sita, found. Then he corrects himself, thinking through the logic: Sita, imprisoned and grieving and refusing Ravana's suit, would not be lying at ease in his own bedchamber dressed in festival ornaments. This woman sleeps here by her own will. This is Mandodari, Ravana's primary queen. Beauty alone had nearly deceived him.

**Cantos XV–XVI (Banquet hall; despair; renewed search):** He searches the banquet hall — musicians, wine vessels, food remains, sleeping women still holding instruments. Still no Sita. He considers: what if she was eaten? He reviews the evidence — Sampati's witness, the dropped ornaments, the months of captivity — and concludes she must be somewhere in Lanka. He begins searching again.

**Cantos XVII–XIX (Ashoka grove; Sita found):** He enters the Ashoka grove — a garden attached to the palace complex, described as the most beautiful enclosed space in the three worlds: golden trees, crystal paths, birds of impossible color. At the base of a Simsapa tree, in shade, a woman — thin, weeping, in rough bark garment, hair braided into a single braid (the braid of a captive), wearing no ornaments except one gem in her hair-knot, surrounded by sleeping she-demons. Her beauty declares itself even in this state. She can only be Sita. Hanuman settles in the tree above her.

**Cantos XX–XXII (Ravana's wooing; Sita's refusals; the ultimatum):** Ravana arrives at the grove with a torch-carrying retinue, repeating his suit — listing Lanka's wealth, his power over gods and demons, the honor she would have as his primary queen. Sita refuses contemptuously, placing a blade of grass between herself and him as a symbolic barrier. Ravana gives his final ultimatum: two months; after that his cooks will prepare her. He leaves. The she-demons circle Sita with threats and entreaties.

**Canto XXIII (Trijata's dream):** An older she-demon named Trijata speaks up. She orders the others to sleep. When they wake she tells her dream: Rama and Lakshmana approaching in white clothes on a white chariot drawn by a white elephant; Ravana shaved, smeared with oil, driven south on a donkey-cart toward Yama's realm; Lanka sinking below the ocean; every demon consumed by fire. She interprets the dream — their race is doomed; Sita should be treated with respect. The she-demons, frightened, comply. Sita thanks Trijata.

**Cantos XXIV–XXVII (Hanuman approaches Sita; gives the ring):** Hanuman deliberates in the Simsapa tree. He must speak without alarming Sita — she will assume any approaching stranger is Ravana in disguise. He decides to use Common Sanskrit, not the High Register; the kind a messenger from a distant kingdom would use. He begins speaking from the tree, recounting Rama's story in order from the beginning. Sita hears and looks up, investigates, sees a small monkey. She is suspicious — Ravana can take any form. Hanuman descends and shows her Rama's ring. She weeps with joy, then immediately suspects illusion. He urges her to accept; she holds the ring against her heart.

**Canto XXVIII (The crow test):** Sita recounts the story of the crow at Chitrakuta — the crow that pecked her feet, the magic arrow, the crow's offered eye — as a secret test. Only someone genuinely from Rama would know this. Hanuman confirms every detail. She is satisfied.

**Canto XXIX (Sita refuses to be carried; the gem):** Hanuman grows to mountain size and offers to carry Sita — one night's travel and she would be with Rama. She refuses: she will not touch any male being but Rama; it is her vow; she will not break it even for rescue. She also fears the sea crossing at speed. She does not doubt Hanuman's strength. She gives him instead a gem — taken from the jeweled plait in her hair, one of Indra's gifts originally given through Anasuyá — as proof for Rama that Hanuman found her.

**Cantos XXX–XXXII (Destruction of the Ashoka grove):** Hanuman decides to cause damage before leaving — to gather intelligence about Lanka's defenses and to demonstrate that the city can be entered by an enemy. He destroys the Ashoka grove entirely: tears trees, breaks fountains, kills the peacocks, shatters every pavilion with his hands and tail. The grove is demolished. She-demons report to Ravana. Ravana sends Jambumali, son of his general Prahasta. Hanuman kills him with a Sal tree used as a club. Ravana sends seven sons of his generals. Part 8 ends mid-battle at Canto XLV as the seven generals attack together.

---

### Part 9 Detailed Content (Lines 1–939 of part9of12.txt)

**Book V continued — Cantos XLVI–XLVIII (The seven generals; Aksha; Indrajit captures Hanuman):** Hanuman kills all five of the seven generals. Ravana sends his young son Aksha — brilliant, car-borne, full of courage — who fights Hanuman in the air, inflicts wounds, is finally seized by his ankles and whirled to death. Ravana sends Indrajit (Meghnad), his greatest warrior and son. Indrajit fires Brahma's serpent-noose, a magic binding spell that Hanuman could have resisted but chose not to, out of reverence for Brahma's weapon. Hanuman is tied with hemp and bark and dragged through the city.

**Cantos XLIX–LII (Hanuman before Ravana; his speech; Vibhishana saves him):** Hanuman observes Ravana closely — notes his magnificence, his scars, his ten heads, his jewels — and reflects that if he honored dharma he could rule all worlds. Ravana's general Prahasta questions Hanuman. Hanuman gives his full account: he came from Rama, he found Sita, he asks Ravana to return her or face destruction. Vibhishana argues against executing Hanuman: ancient law forbids killing envoys. Ravana agrees to a symbolic punishment instead.

**Canto LIII (The burning tail; Sita's prayer; Hanuman escapes):** Ravana orders Hanuman's tail bound with cotton strips soaked in oil and set alight. The giants parade him through Lanka's streets while crowds cheer. Sita learns of this and prays to fire directly: if she has kept her vows, spare Hanuman. The flames fail to burn him. Hanuman shrinks, slips his bonds, seizes a club, kills the nearest giants, escapes to the rooftops.

**Canto LIV (Hanuman burns Lanka):** Hanuman, still trailing fire, leaps from palace to palace setting each alight. The golden buildings burn. Women flee to rooftops. He maps the city as he burns it. He spares Vibhishana's house deliberately. Drums and shells sound disaster. Lanka's glory is partially destroyed in one night.

**Canto LV (Hanuman's remorse; heavenly confirmation):** Cooling on a rooftop, Hanuman is struck by horror: he set fire to the entire city and Sita was somewhere in it. He considers drowning himself, entering the flames, going south to the ocean. Then voices from the sky — the gods and sages who have been watching — cry out: "Sita lives. No flame harmed a hair of her head." He is consoled and returns to say farewell to Sita.

**Cantos LVI–LVII (Farewell; Hanuman's return; the honey grove):** Hanuman tells Sita he is returning to Rama. She gives him a message — the Chitrakuta crow story again, as confirmation — and blessings. He departs, leaping back across the ocean. He finds the south search party at the honey grove of Sugriva's uncle Dadhimukha; the Vanars, drunk with excitement, have rifled the grove; Dadhimukha goes to Sugriva in complaint; Sugriva infers from the revelry that the search succeeded and bids the party return quickly.

**Canto LXV (The gem; Hanuman's report; Rama's lament on the gem):** Hanuman gives Sita's gem to Rama. Rama presses it to his chest and weeps — it is Janaka's wedding gift, given through Indra, worn on her brow. He gives Hanuman's full report: Sita lives, weeps, gives Rama one month before she can no longer sustain herself. Rama's grief speech: he wants Hanuman to describe every word she said, because her words alone can give him the will to live.

**Book VI, Cantos I–IV (Book VI begins; Sugriva's speech; Hanuman's description of Lanka; the march):** Rama's immediate question: how to cross the sea. Sugriva argues that grief is the only obstacle — the Vánars can destroy any army, and the enemy's location is known; they must act. Hanuman describes Lanka's defenses in detail: golden walls, four fortified gates, each gate held by thousands, moats full of monsters, internal walls of coral and ivory, and the Pushpaka car. He names specific captains for each gate. Rama gives march orders: Nila in the van; specific chieftains on each flank; Rama in the center on Hanuman's back; Lakshmana on Angad. The army moves south, filling the plain "like a second sea." They reach the ocean.

**Cantos V–VIII (Rama's lament at the shore; Ravana's council):** Rama stands on the shore and laments — every cloud looks like Sita struggling in Ravana's arms; he would lie down in the deep to cool his grief. Ravana holds a council after Hanuman's damage. His generals boast they will destroy Rama easily. Ravana gives his own assessment: he is secure from gods, demons, and serpents by Brahma's boon; men and Vanars were not specified; he is worried. Kumbhakarna says Ravana was a fool to steal the wife first and ask counsel after — but now that it's done, he'll fight.

**Cantos IX–X, XIV–XVI (Vibhishana's three counsels; the debate):** Vibhishana gives Ravana three formal speeches over two separate council sessions, each urging Sita's return. He lists the omens — cattle without milk, sacred fires won't kindle, snakes in the altar, trembling elephants. He argues that Rama cannot be defeated, Kumbhakarna cannot stop him, Indrajit cannot stop him. Prahasta and Indrajit ridicule Vibhishana. Ravana calls him a traitor to his race. Vibhishana, in front of the full court, gives Ravana his final reply: he is warning him as a brother, and Rama's arrow will kill him before it's done.

**Cantos XVII–XIX (Vibhishana's flight; the Vanar debate; Rama accepts the suppliant):** Vibhishana flies from Lanka with four attendants and lands in the air over the Vanar army on the shore. The Vanars panic — a giant descending from above, they assume hostile. Vibhishana declares himself from the air. Sugriva immediately suspects a spy. The Vanar chieftains debate: Angad says don't trust him; Jambavan says suspect any ally of Ravana; Hanuman alone argues for trust. Rama quotes the story of the dove who fed the fowler who killed his mate, offering its own flesh as hospitality — the ancient law of shelter for suppliants. "Even if this were Ravana himself, I could not turn him away." He welcomes Vibhishana and, at Vibhishana's suggestion, has Lakshmana pour ocean water over his head — Vibhishana is crowned king of Lanka in anticipation.

**Cantos XX–XXII (Ocean threatened; Nala builds the bridge):** Rama lies on the shore for three days fasting, asking Ocean to grant passage. Ocean doesn't come. Rama's fury — he will dry the ocean with Brahma's weapon. He takes the arrow. The earth shakes, animals flee, stars fall. Ocean appears — rising from the water in golden light attended by every river. He says: I cannot leave a pathway through myself, but I will hold still and let you build. He tells Rama where to fire the Brahma arrow (at the Abhira thieves on a distant northern shore). Nala — son of the divine architect Vishvakarma through his mother's boon — declares himself and says he will build the bridge. In five days the Vanar army throws rocks, mountains, and trees into the sea; Nala directs placement; the bridge solidifies, 100 leagues long, wide enough for tens of millions to march.

**The crossing; reaching Lanka's shore (Cantos XXIII–XXIV):** The entire army crosses. Bad omens as they march — blood raining from clouds, meteors, clouds the shape of cannibals. Rama assigns positions on Lanka's perimeter: Nila at the east gate (Prahasta's post), Angad at the south gate (Mahodar's), Hanuman at the west gate (Indrajit's), Rama and Lakshmana at the north (Ravana's post). The army closes the ring around Lanka.

**Cantos XXV–XXX (Ravana's spies; the magic head):** Ravana sends Shuka and Sarana in Vanar disguise to count the armies. Vibhishana spots them immediately. Rama releases them and tells them to report back: Ravana has two choices — return Sita or die. Sarana and Shuka identify each Vanar chieftain from the wall to Ravana; Ravana dismisses them angrily. He sends Sardula, who is also caught; reports in terror that the army cannot be counted. Ravana — his private fear now showing — summons the magician Vidyujjihva and has him create a perfect replica of Rama's severed head, complete with his bow and arrows. He brings this to Sita in the Ashoka grove and tells her Prahasta killed Rama in a night attack on the sleeping camp. Sita weeps over the severed head; collapses; mourns in detail; when Prahasta summons Ravana away, the magic head disappears — it was illusion.

**Canto XXXIII–XXXIV (Sarama's truth):** Sarama, one of the she-demon guards who genuinely loves Sita, had hidden in the thicket and heard the entire exchange with Ravana. She tells Sita the truth: the head was a deception; Rama is alive; his armies are at Lanka's walls; she can hear the drums of preparation from here. She offers to fly to Rama for Sita and return. Sita asks her instead to discover Ravana's plans. Sarama returns with intelligence: Ravana's own mother begged him to return Sita; the senior nobles supported her; Ravana refused. His course is set.

**Canto XXXV (Malyavan's counsel; the omens):** Ravana's maternal grandfather Malyavan — an elder of great wisdom — speaks one final time. He lists the omens of Lanka's destruction: dogs in the shrine eating sacred oil, cats pairing with tigers, asses born of cows, great clouds of blood-rain, jackals howling at every gate, sacred fires guttering. He argues that Vishnu himself has taken Rama's form, and that no giant can defeat the divine. Ravana dismisses him contemptuously and sends him from the hall.

**Canto XXXVI (Ravana's defiance):** Ravana's full defiance speech: Why would he fear a man who travels with monkeys? Can I, feared by all the gods, tremble at a forest exile? He also explains why he hasn't forced Sita — the curse from Brahma: if he ravishes a woman against her will again, his head will split in pieces. He is constrained by this curse, not by ethics.

**Cantos XXXVII–XLI (Gate assignments; Sugriva attacks Ravana on the wall; Angad's embassy):** Vibhishana delivers intelligence on all four gate placements. Rama assigns each gate to a chieftain and assigns himself the north where Ravana commands. Then — impulsively — Sugriva leaps from Suvela's peak to Ravana's watchtower and physically attacks Ravana, snatching his crown and hurling it down. They grapple on the tower, fall into the moat, fight in the moat, both wounded; Sugriva leaps back to his own lines; Rama rebukes him. Rama sends Angad as formal envoy to Ravana's court. Angad delivers Rama's ultimatum; Ravana orders him seized; four giants grab him; Angad carries them to the palace roof, hurls them down, and leaps back.

**Cantos XLII–XLVI (The battle opens; Indrajit's serpent-noose):** The full battle begins — walls assaulted, gates attacked. Single combats: Indrajit vs Angad (Angad tears his bow and wrecks his chariot); Hanuman kills Jambumali; Lakshmana kills Virupaksha. Night falls; the battle continues in darkness — recognition by voice, not sight. Indrajit, invisible in battle-mist, fires arrows that are transformed snakes — they bind Rama and Lakshmana in a living noose no god can loose. The brothers fall. The Vánars gather around the fallen pair in grief and keep watch.

**Cantos XLVII–L (Sita shown the bodies; Trijata; Garuda heals the brothers):** Ravana puts Sita in his flying car and takes her over the battlefield to see the fallen brothers. Sita's lament. Trijata reads the signs — the brothers' faces still glowing, the armies not dispersed — and tells Sita these men are not dead. Rama wakes first; laments over Lakshmana's apparent death. Then Garuda — the cosmic eagle, Vishnu's vehicle — descends with a roar that drives all serpents underground. The serpent-arrows dissolve. Garuda touches the brothers' faces; their wounds close; their strength returns doubled. Garuda departs. The Vanar army roars.

**Cantos LI–LIX (Major generals fall; Ravana's first sally):** Ravana sends Dhumraksha — Hanuman kills him with a mountain peak; then Vajradanshtra — Angad kills him after a prolonged single combat with fists, swords, and shields. The text summarizes (with editorial omission notes) Akampana's and Prahasta's deaths — Hanuman kills Akampana, Nila kills Prahasta. Then Ravana himself sallies out in his magnificent war-car. He wounds Sugriva badly. He fights Hanuman (they wound each other). He fights Lakshmana — fires Brahma's lance, which fells Lakshmana badly; then seizes Lakshmana and tries to lift him bodily from the earth, but Lakshmana is too heavy (divine will). Hanuman knocks Ravana down with his fist. Ráma rides Hanuman into combat against Ravana — destroys Ravana's chariot, kills the horses, kills the charioteer, shatters Ravana's bow, strikes the crown from his head. Ravana stands disarmed and disheveled. Rama says: "You are weary from today's fight. Go rest in Lanka and return tomorrow — then I will kill you." Ravana retreats in humiliation.

**Canto LX (Kumbhakarna roused):** Ravana returns to Lanka, admits internally that Brahma's prophecy is being fulfilled — the man he was not protected against, Rama, is indeed the threat. He recalls all the prophecies of his doom from various cursed women and sages. He orders Kumbhakarna — his enormous brother who sleeps for six months at a time by Brahma's curse — to be roused. Part 9 ends as the waking effort begins.

---

### Part 10 Detailed Content

**Cantos LXI–LXVII (Kumbhakarna's sally and death):** The Rakshasas finally rouse Kumbhakarna from his six-month sleep with enormous amounts of food and drink. He is a giant who darkens the sky — his boast is that he will eat the Vanar army and drink the sea. He sallies out; the monkey army flees in terror. Sugriva alone stands, fights Kumbhakarna directly — is captured and squeezed under the giant's arm; Sugriva bites off his nose and ear and escapes. Lakshmana wounds Kumbhakarna with many shafts. Then Rama kills him with a systematic sequence of specifically named divine arrows: the Wind-God's dart (severing the right arm with its mace), Indra's dart (severing the left arm), two crescent-headed arrows (severing both legs), and finally the Brahma-given arrow (decapitation). The severed head, enormous as a mountain, crashes through a city gate as it falls. Ravana laments his brother.

**Cantos LXVIII–LXXII (Ravana's sons fall):** Ravana sends his sons: Narantaka sallies on horseback and is killed by Angad (who tears off the horse's head and strikes him with it). Trisiras and Devantak and Mahaparsva sally — Hanuman and Nila kill them. Atikaya, enormous and near-invincible, is killed by Lakshmana with a Brahma-given wind weapon after his body proves impervious to ordinary arrows. Ravana's lamentations deepen; he now has no sons.

**Indrajit's second binding (editorial summary):** Griffith's editorial note acknowledges that at this point in the text he has omitted Canto LXXIII (Indrajit's false Sita effigy) in full because of its length and the difficulty of translating it faithfully. The full canto in Valmiki describes Indrajit bringing a magical illusory Sita in his chariot to the battlefield and cutting her down before Hanuman's eyes. Hanuman, who had met the real Sita days before, is devastated — he and the entire Vanar army believe she is dead. Rama and Lakshmana collapse in grief. It is illusion: the real Sita is still alive and weeping in the Ashoka grove. Griffith summarizes the episode in a bracketed note and proceeds.

**Cantos LXXIV–LXXVIII (Indrajit's fire sacrifice and death — partly editorial):** Indrajit retreats to perform a fire sacrifice that will give him invincibility for the coming day's battle. Lakshmana, on Vibhishana's guidance, interrupts the rite before it is completed — Griffith notes the full account is again summarized, compressed from many cantos. In Griffith's version, Lakshmana and Indrajit fight at length; Lakshmana finally uses a Brahma-given arrow and kills Indrajit. Ravana's lament for Indrajit is the most anguished of all his grief-speeches — Indrajit was his greatest general, and Ravana provided for Indrajit's education in sorcery and warfare from childhood.

**Kumbha, Nikumbha, Makarakshe (editorial summaries):** Griffith notes in brackets the omission of cantos on Kumbha and Nikumbha (Kumbhakarna's sons) — Sugriva kills Kumbha; Hanuman kills Nikumbha. Makarakshe is killed by Rama (editorial summary of a canto omitted). Night attack on Lanka follows.

**Cantos LXXIX–LXXXV (Ravana's final sally; Lakshmana falls; Rama and the Sun Hymn):** Ravana himself sallies in his chariot for the last great battle. He wounds Lakshmana with Maya's magical lance — Lakshmana falls senseless. Hanuman is ordered to the Himalayas for life-restoring herbs; the mountain peak defeats Hanuman's ability to identify the specific plants, so he lifts the entire mountain and carries it back. The physician Sushena revives Lakshmana with the herbs. Indra sends his divine chariot with Matali as charioteer to Rama's side. The sage Agastya appears (descends from heaven) and recites the Aditya Hridayam — the Sun Hymn — to Rama before the final battle begins, instructing him that reciting it three times will grant him victory.

**Cantos LXXXVI–LXXXIX (The death of Ravana):** Rama and Ravana fight at enormous length — an equal battle at first; every time Rama cuts off one of Ravana's heads, it regenerates. Matali whispers that Rama must use the Brahma-given arrow. Rama fires it — feathered with wind, its point made of fire and sun, its weight drawn from Meru and Mandar, given to Agastya by Brahma, then given to Rama. It passes through Ravana's chest and enters the earth. Ravana dies. The gods praise Rama from the sky.

**Cantos XC–XCII (Funeral rites; Vibhishana crowned):** Vibhishana at first refuses to perform Ravana's funeral rites, citing his brother's sins. Rama rebukes him: enmity ends with death; Ravana was a great king and warrior; the rites must be done. Mandodari's lament is extended and profound — she alone among the mourners seems to understand that Rama was Vishnu incarnate; she says she guesses that is why Ravana was doomed from the start, not because he was weak but because a god had decided to be born as a man. Vibhishana is then crowned king of Lanka by Rama.

**Cantos XCIII–XCVII (Sita's test and vindication):** Sita is brought before Rama. Rama delivers a long speech publicly rejecting her — he says he fought for his name and the fame of his house, not for her; that a woman who has dwelt in another man's house cannot be taken back; that she is free to go where she will. Sita's reply is dignified and controlled; she notes she was taken by force, not by will; she compares herself to gold that can be tested by fire. She asks Lakshmana to build a pyre. She enters the fire, praying to Fire God (Agni) directly. Fire God carries her out unharmed and certifies her purity before the assembled gods and sages. Brahma and the other Gods descend and confirm that Rama is Vishnu incarnate, that he had to deny Sita publicly so the world's eyes would see the proof. Dasharatha descends from heaven to bless his sons. Indra grants a great boon: all the Vanars who died in the war are restored to life.

**Cantos XCVIII–CII (The return; Rama's consecration):** The party travels home on the Pushpaka sky-car — Sita narrates the geography below (pointing out Lanka, the bridge, the sea, Adam's Bridge, Kishkindha) and Rama describes each place they pass. Hanuman races ahead to announce the arrival to Bharat; Bharat faints with joy. Grand procession; the entire kingdom comes out. Rama is consecrated king of Ayodhya. The closing hymn describes ten thousand years of Rama's reign — no child dies before a parent, no disease, no sin, great crops and rain.

**Appendix — Ravana Doomed (Section XIII):** Griffith includes in four languages (Carey+Marshman English prose, Schlegel Latin, Gorresio Italian, Fauche French) the council of the gods where Brahma goes before Vishnu to beg him to incarnate as a mortal to kill Ravana. Ravana had earned a boon that made him immune from being killed by any god, demon, serpent, or divine creature — he had omitted to ask protection from men, thinking men too weak to threaten him. The four translations show the same scene through four different scholarly lenses.

**Uttarakanda summary (Additional Notes):** Griffith appends a summary of the seventh book, which is considered by some scholars to be a later addition. It contains: Ravana's genealogy (grandson of Brahma through Pulastya; his race's claim to Lanka preceded Kubera's); the original Rakshasa kings and how Lanka was given to them; Kumbhakarna's curse explained (he asked for eternal sleep as a boon, accidentally, because Saraswati confused his tongue at the crucial moment). Mandodari's origin. Ravana conquering Indra. The Vedavati story — a woman of great piety whom Ravana abducted; she cursed him and burned herself; she was reborn as Sita, the cause of his death. Sita's second exile — Rama, knowing she is pure, sends her away from Ayodhya because the people of the city talk and doubt. She gives birth to Kusa and Lava in Valmiki's hermitage. At the Ashvamedha (horse sacrifice), Kusa and Lava recite the full Ramayana before the assembled court including their unknowing father. Sita, invited to return to Ayodhya and prove her innocence before the assembly, instead calls on the Earth goddess to receive her — and the earth opens and swallows her. Rama enters the Sarayu river and ascends to heaven. Additional dictionary entries and a lengthy essay by Gorresio on the theological differences between Hindu avatar doctrine and Christian Incarnation close the volume.

Part 10 ends with the full Ramayana narrative complete, including the Uttarakanda and scholarly apparatus.

---

### Part 11 Detailed Content

Part 11 is entirely scholarly apparatus — no new narrative. It contains:

**Uttarakanda narrative fragment (lines 1–22):** A brief verse passage from Kalidasa's Raghuvamsha amplifying the Uttarakanda: Rama sees Ayodhya from a high seat and calls his spy Bhadra for a report. Bhadra reluctantly reveals that the people of Ayodhya praise all Rama's deeds but one — they blame him for taking Sita back after her captivity. Rama is devastated. He banishes Sita. Lakshmana takes her to Valmiki's grove and reveals the command; Sita swoons, then delivers a message for Rama through Lakshmana ("say, was it worthy of thy noble race…"). Valmiki shelters her. That same night Sita gives birth to Kusa and Lava. They grow up under Valmiki, learn the Vedas, and are taught to sing the Ramayana. They wander singing it; Rama hears them and sees his own face in their faces; Valmiki confirms they are his sons.

**Additional Notes (lines 23–637):** Griffith's scholarly apparatus. Major items:

- *Parashurama:* He cleared the earth of the Kshatriya caste seventeen times, filling five lakes with their blood; retired to Mount Mahendra where he still resides; the episode may be interpolated to have him confirm Rama as Vishnu.
- *Fate (Indian vs. Greek):* Gorresio's note distinguishing Indian karma-fate (consequence of past lives, linked to metempsychosis) from Greek moira (inexorable external power).
- *Vishvamitra's caste-breaking:* He was a king who conquered Brahmanhood by penance — an unprecedented rupture of caste; the Vedas mention only his rivalry with Vasishtha as a political/priestly contest.
- *The Suppliant Dove (full ballad, W. Waterfield):* Kasi's king shelters a dove fleeing a hawk. The hawk demands the dove as his rightful prey. The king offers a cow, a deer, a ram — the hawk refuses: his diet is dove only, and he has a right to his prey. They negotiate: the hawk will take an equal weight of the king's own flesh instead. The king cuts flesh from his side, arm, chest, back, thigh — the scale never balances; the dove always outweighs the flesh. He throws himself bodily in the scale. The gods descend and restore him. This is described as the origin of the king's obligation to shelter suppliants at any personal cost.
- *Consecration ceremony (coronation rites):* Full scholarly description from Goldstücker's Dictionary of Rama's coronation implements: golden water-jars, throne, tiger-skin chariot, water from the Ganges-Jumna confluence and all sacred rivers, honey, curd, ghee, grain, grass, flowers, milk, eight beautiful damsels, a splendid elephant, golden/silver jars with Udumbara branches and lotuses, white jeweled chourie, white parasol, white bull, white horse, all instruments, bards; also scimitar, bow, litter, golden vase, blazing fire, gaudy courtesans, professors of divinity, Brahmanas, cows, pure beasts and birds, chiefs of town and country.
- *Vritra (three-scholar comparative entry):* Gorresio, Cox, and Chambers' Cyclopaedia all interpret Vritra as the cloud withholding rain — the primordial conflict between sky-god (Indra) and storm/darkness (Vritra); compared to Zeus vs Typhon, Apollo vs Python, Bellerophon vs Chimaera, Sigurd vs Fafnir.
- *The Vanar host as solar mythology (De Gubernatis):* Extensive comparative essay: Bali = old sun/Indra, Sugriva = new spring sun/Vishnu; Hanuman = sun entering cloud, his tail setting Lanka on fire = morning rays destroying darkness; also compared to Samson (bound with cords, could escape, but did not; his distinctive feature, his tail, is targeted for burning).
- *Wheeler's essays:* Rama's alliance with Sugriva was "with the wrong party" — Bali's legal right was superior; Rama killed Bali "by an act contrary to all the laws of fair fighting"; Tara's transfer from Bali to Sugriva condoned by Rama matches the marriage customs of the Marawars (supposed aboriginal Carnatic people). Wheeler also argues the Vanar army = aboriginal forest tribes, not literal monkeys.
- *Gorresio's conclusion (Preface to Vol. X):* Ravana's race was a "black and ferocious" people of Ceylon/Lanka with woolly hair, thick lips, love of gold ornaments, worship of force — they match accounts of the historical Hamitic races; the war was real, not allegory. Extended argument: the Ramayana cannot be purely allegorical because no 50,000-verse epic built on pure allegory could have lived in living memory across all of India's dialects for 3,000 years. Compared to the "Napoleon is the sun" analogy — the scholar who proved Napoleon allegorical by noting he was born on an island, traveled west to east, had twelve marshals = twelve signs of the zodiac. The comparison is a reductio ad absurdum of excessive allegorism.
- *Literal translation of the Aditya Hridayam:* The Sun Hymn Agastya gave to Rama. The literal translation lists all of the Sun's identities with commentary: he is Brahma, Vishnu, Shiva, Skanda, Prajapati, Mahendra, Dhanada, Kala, Yama, Soma, Pitris, Vasus, Sadhyas, Ashvins, Maruts, Manu, Vayu, Agni, Prajā, Prana — then 108 specific epithets (Aditya, Savita, Surya, Khaga, Pushan, etc.) each with meaning: "He who dries all things," "He who destroys darkness," "The most glorious of all that are glorious," "One who is identical with the twelve months," etc. Three recitations of this hymn gave Rama victory over Ravana.

**Index of Principal Names (lines 638–2375):** Alphabetical index of all persons, places, and concepts in the translation with page references. ~1500 lines. Useful for cross-referencing but contains no narrative.

**Numbered endnotes, notes 1–355 (lines 2375–3186):** Scholarly footnotes. Notable items:
- Note 233 (Schlegel on the weapon-battle between Vasishtha and Vishvamitra): the contest is an allegory of the struggle between priestly and military authority, "like one of those struggles which our own Europe saw in the middle ages when without employing warlike weapons the priesthood frequently gained the victory."
- Note 265 (Rahu): the ascending node is in mythology a demon whose head was severed by Vishnu but being immortal retained separate existence; head and tail both transferred to the stellar sphere, causing eclipses by swallowing sun and moon.
- Note 276 (King Yayati's lease on heaven): only the highest merit earns eternal heaven; minor merit earns only a terminable lease, which expires and the soul is "unceremoniously ejected."

Part 11 ends at footnote 355.

---

### Part 12 Detailed Content

Part 12 is entirely scholarly apparatus — no narrative content whatsoever. It contains:

**Numbered endnotes, notes 356–1184 (lines 1–1714):** Continuation of Part 11's footnotes. Notable items:
- Notes 1073–1184 are the full commentary on the Aditya Hridayam (Sun Hymn) — each of the 108 epithets is given a Sanskrit explanation. Examples: note 1092 "Prajā — One who creates all beings"; note 1126 "Tamobhedī — One who destroys darkness"; note 1142 "Dwādaśātman — One who is identical with the twelve months." The commentary makes clear that the hymn was understood as a complete cosmological taxonomy, not mere praise.
- Note 1183: "Not only have the races of India translated or epitomized it [the Ramayana], but foreign nations have appropriated it wholly or in part, Persia, Java, and Japan itself."
- Note 1184: Megasthenes visited India "in the third century B.C." — cited in Gorresio's argument about the Ramayana's antiquity.
- Notes 355 (Schlegel): on the end of Part 11 — pillars of the Indra-worship banner.

**Project Gutenberg license boilerplate (lines 1720–1827):** Standard PG license text. No content.

Part 12 ends at line 1827 with the full Project Gutenberg license. The Ramayana source text is now fully read: all 12 parts complete.
